Diffuse Adiabatic Flows in Thermally Coupled Grounded Shallow Ice Sheets: Modelling and Analysis
Paolo Piersanti

Abstract
. In this article we propose a novel thermodynamical model which couples the evolution of the thickness of a grounded shallow ice sheet with the evolution of its internal temperature. Both the grounded shallow ice sheet surface elevation and the ice internal temperature are subjected to physical constraints. The equations governing the evolution of the grounded shallow ice sheet thickness are degenerate, and the ice internal temperature evolves in a moving domain. First, we formally model the phenomenon under consideration by adopting strategies akin to those employed in the construction of diffuse-interface models. Second, we establish the existence of solutions for one such formal model by means of the penalty method, and we observe that the low regularity of the problem under consideration prevents us from obtaining a standard concept of solution.



August 10, 2026

1.
Introduction

The mathematical modelling of ice sheets is fundamentally characterised by the interaction between a free boundary, governing the ice thickness, and physical fields, such as temperature, defined on the evolving domain itself.

In this article we propose and study a mathematical model describing the interaction between the evolution of the thickness of a grounded shallow ice sheet (from now on, simply
shallow ice sheet
) and the evolution of the ice internal temperature within it.

Literature Review

The rigorous mathematical study of models in Glaciology dates back, to our best knowledge, to 2002, when Calvo, Díaz, Durany, Schiavi and Vázquez
[
15
]
published a paper discussing the evolution of the thickness of a shallow ice sheet as an obstacle problem. In their article, these authors only considered
one spatial direction
, they assumed the basal velocity to be smooth, and assumed the bedrock to be flat. This formulation was also exploited to justify the generation of fast ice streams in ice sheets flowing along soft and deformable beds
[
26
]
. In this direction, we also cite the related paper
[
25
]
by Díaz and his collaborators.

Ten years later, in 2012, Jouvet & Bueler
[
49
]
studied the steady (i.e., time-independent) version of the problem considered in
[
15
]
where, this time, two spatial directions and a more general bedrock topography were taken into account.

In 2023, in
[
63
]
, the time-dependent version of the model considered in
[
49
]
was mathematically analysed, under the assumption that the bedrock was flat. The latter assumption is physically realistic in the context of many ice sheets (e.g., the Greenland ice sheet
[
4
]
and the Devon ice cap, described as a dome-shaped ice mass resting atop a bedrock plateau
[
27
]
). The elliptic bulk energy for the model considered in
[
49
]
is expressed in terms of a tilted
p
-Laplacian, which is in general non-monotone. Requiring the bedrock to be flat restores the monotonicity and ensured the viability of the mathematical analysis of the time-dependent version of the model studied in
[
49
]
by means of monotonicity methods
[
10
,
53
]
.

Numerical studies in ice sheet dynamics were carried out by Hughes & Ghattas and their collaborators in a series of papers. For instance, in
[
61
]
, Hughes & Ghattas and their collaborators proposed an infinite-dimensional adjoint-based inexact Gauss-Newton method for the solution of inverse problems governed by Stokes models of ice sheet flow with non-linear rheology and sliding law. In
[
46
]
, motivated by the need for efficient and accurate simulation of the dynamics of the polar ice sheets, Ghattas and his collaborators designed scalable solvers for the solution of non-linear incompressible Stokes equations. In
[
45
]
, Ghattas and his collaborators proposed efficient and scalable algorithms for this end-to-end, data-to-prediction process under the Gaussian approximation and in the context of modelling the flow of the Antarctic ice sheet and its effect on loss of grounded ice to the ocean. In
[
74
]
, Hughes & Ghattas and their collaborators studied inverse problems to recover the geothermal heat flux starting from ice flow observations.

Stemming from the analysis presented in
[
63
]
, numerical studies by Chawla, Holmes and Temam followed, some of which employed Physics-Informed Neural Networks to overcome the difficulties owing to the lack of regularity of solutions of the governing model
[
16
,
17
]
.

Grounded ice sheets and sea ice are two related but physically different problems. The mathematical literature dealing with sea ice and glaciers has been growing rapidly since the early 2000s; in this direction we mention, for instance, the articles
[
47
,
48
,
50
,
51
,
55
,
68
,
69
]
, and the landmark paper by W.D. Hibler
[
42
]
on sea ice modelling. The local-in-time well-posedness of Hibler’s model has recently been established in the article by Liu, Thomas & Titi
[
54
]
. The authors designed a regularizing scheme based on physical observations for deriving the sought local-in-time well-posedness.

In
[
9
]
, Hieber and his collaborators investigated the local-in-time well-posedness of Hibler’s model by means of a different regularisation approach. Precisely, the authors constructed the local strong solutions of the corresponding regularised system by investigating the analytic semi-group property of the parabolic operator of the regularised system in a bounded domain, and they also established the global well-posedness in time for initial data close to constant equilibria.
In this direction, we also mention the recent work by Hieber’s research group
[
7
,
6
,
8
]
.

In the recent paper
[
32
]
, Figalli, Ros-Oton & Serra studied the phase transition of ice melting to water as a Stefan problem. The results established in
[
32
]
provide a refined understanding of the Stefan problem’s singularities and answer some long-standing open questions in the field of free-boundary problems.

Novelties and main results

The purpose of this paper is to extend the study initiated in
[
63
]
to the case where the evolution of the ice internal temperature is coupled with the evolution of the ice thickness. The
main point of innovation
introduced here consists in considering the coupling between the ice thickness evolution and the internal ice temperature evolution. Since the ice thickness level is constrained to remain on or above the bedrock at all times, the variational problem describing the evolution of the ice thickness can be formulated as a
time-dependent obstacle problem
.

On the one hand, the ice thickness of a shallow ice sheet evolves as a consequence of a manifold of factors like, for instance, the rate at which snow deposits, the rate at which melting occurs, as well as the velocity at which the glacier slides along the bedrock.

On the other hand, the ice internal temperature evolves as a result of the temperature of the surrounding environment, the heat irradiated through the bedrock surface, and the effective stress exerted by the ice mass.
According to Section 5.4 in
[
41
]
, the governing equations coupling the evolution of ice thickness and the evolution of ice internal temperature and the boundary conditions change if the ice internal temperature either drops below the
pressure melting point
or reaches the pressure melting point, developing
latent heat
.

In the context of this analysis, we consider the case where the ice bedrock is flat (as in
[
63
]
) and
frozen
, in the sense that the ice internal temperature is constrained to remain below the pressure melting point. Also in this case, the variational problem associated with the evolution of the ice internal temperature can be formulated as a time-dependent obstacle problem.

The problem under consideration, in addition to being novel from both the modelling point of view and the analytical point of view, introduces challenges owing to the fact that the ice internal temperature is defined in the portion of space occupied by ice. This means that, in addition to having to deal with non-linearities associated with the presence of a
free boundary
, for both the shallow ice sheet thickness and the ice internal temperature, substantial caution must be exercised as the governing equations for the ice internal temperature are posed over a
moving domain
, where the motion of the domain boundary is expressed in terms of the ice thickness, which is one of the unknowns of the problem under consideration.
To summarise, the
main novelties
presented in this paper are the following:

(1)

This paper introduces (viz. Section
2
) reduction techniques for transforming the original model coupling the evolution of the shallow ice sheet thickness with the evolution of the ice internal temperature (cf., e.g., Section 5.4 in
[
41
]
) to an equivalent model posed over a
fixed
domain. These reduction techniques render the problem more tractable from the mathematical point of view at the cost of considering an approximation of the temperature evolution that vanishes near the ice surface. From the point of view of Physics, this feature characterises ice sheets covered with a continuum layer of debris. The latter feature of the thermal model, which is observable in the Miage ice sheet and the Karakorum ice sheet
[
56
,
57
,
60
,
66
]
, justifies the choice for the adjective
adiabatic
in the title of this paper (cf. Remark
3.3
);

(2)

This paper establishes the existence of
very weak solutions
of the initial boundary-value problem associated with the
reduced model
obtained upon completion of item
(1)
, coupling the evolution of the shallow ice sheet surface with the evolution of the ice internal temperature, in the case where both the unknown magnitudes are subjected to obeying or geometrical or physical constraints, i.e., the ice thickness is constrained to be greater than zero and the ice internal temperature is constrained to remain below the pressure melting point;

(3)

Beyond its glaciological application, this work introduces a new analytical framework for a class of coupled obstacle problems posed on moving domains.

The main result

The main result of this paper is
Theorem
8.1
, where we establish the
existence of very weak solutions
for the initial boundary-value problem coupling the evolution of the shallow ice sheet thickness with the evolution of its ice internal temperature in the case where the
bedrock is frozen and flat
. In order to do so, we will need to improve the energy estimates in
[
63
]
by
removing
the extra
decrease assumption

(4.6)
made there. In the context of the derivation of the rigorous concept of solution, in
[
63
]
extra regularity assumptions on the weak limit were resorted to. In this paper, we drop this extra regularity assumptions and we obtain an
abstract
limit model. This conclusion is consistent with the results obtained in
[
63
]
where extra regularity had been assumed, while the solution concept aligns with the non-standard framework for evolutionary variational inequalities governed by pseudo-monotone operators
[
62
]
.

Structure of the paper

This paper is divided into eight sections (including this one). In Section
2
we present a formal derivation of the model governing the evolution of the shallow ice sheet surface when the contribution of the ice internal temperature is taken into account.

In Section
3
we present a formal derivation of the model governing the ice temperature evolution, which takes the form of a
moving boundary problem
, where the moving boundary is associated with the solution of the model presented in Section
2
.

In Section
4
we formulate the “penalised” version of the variational problem introduced in Section
2
and Section
3
, and we establish the existence of solutions for the time discretisation for this model. We also present new results that will be employed to study the
ice softness term
coupling the model governing the evolution of the shallow ice sheet surface with the model governing the evolution of the ice internal temperature.

In Section
5
we establish the validity of suitable
a priori
estimates that will allow us to resort to compactness methods.

In Section
6
we establish other preparatory lemmas, that descend from the
a priori
estimates derived in Section
5
.

In Section
7
, we pass to the limit as the time step tends to zero in the discrete model introduced in Section
4
, and we establish the existence of solutions for the “penalised” version of the
formal
obstacle problems introduced in Section
2
and Section
3
.

Finally, in Section
8
, we pass to the limit as the penalty parameter tends to zero in the “penalised” version of the
formal
obstacle problems introduced in Section
2
and Section
3
, and we recover a
rigorous concept of solution
for the model corresponding we formally derived at the end of Section
2
and Section
3
.

-dimensional Euclidean space
(\mathbb{R}^{N},\cdot)

X

\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{X^{\prime},X}

A
Lipschitz domain in

of

, where

, where

denotes an arbitrary time instant.

2.
Formal derivation of the shallow ice sheet surface evolution model

The
bedrock elevation
is described by the function
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{b}{{{}}b}:\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
. We assume that the bedrock topography remains constant over the observation period and, in agreement with (5.85) in
[
41
]
, that
b\equiv 0
in
\overline{\Omega}
up to changing the coordinate system. The latter assumption is applicable to the Greenland ice sheet
[
49
]
and the Devon ice cap
[
16
,
17
]
.

The
ice surface elevation
is given by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{h}{{{}}h}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}
, which immediately implies that the following constraint has be taken into account:

(2.1)

h\geq 0\quad\textup{ in }[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}.

This property frames the problem of ice thickness evolution as an obstacle problem, with the bedrock acting as the
obstacle
and, for this particular choice of the framework, it results that the ice surface elevation coincides with the ice thickness.

We recall that in the Shallow Ice Approximation regime, the ice surface elevation is small in the bulk and might exhibit large slopes at the ice margins, leading to potential degeneracies in the governing equations
[
41
,
44
]
.
The presence of this constraint introduces a
free boundary
(see
[
10
,
14
,
31
]
). For all, or almost every,
t\in(0,T)
, we define the set

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Omega+}{{{}}\Omega_{t}^{+}}:=\{x\in\Omega;h(t,x)>0\},

which represents the
region of
\Omega
covered by ice at time
t
. The associated
free boundary
is the set:

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Gammaft}{{{}}\Gamma_{f,t}}:=\Omega\cap\partial\Omega_{t}^{+}.

The evolution of the ice thickness
h
is driven by the
surface-mass balance

a

s

(accumulation/ablation rate) and the
basal melting rate

a

b

. The function
a_{b}
is zero when the basal temperature is below the pressure melting point and positive otherwise. Typically,
a_{s}
and
a_{b}
depend on the horizontal position
x
and the surface elevation
h(t,x)
, i.e., they are defined as follows:

\displaystyle a_{s}

\displaystyle:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\to\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle a_{b}

\displaystyle:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}.

Since for the problem under consideration the temperature is constrained not to rise above the pressure melting point (cf., Section 5.4 in
[
41
]
), it results
a_{b}\equiv 0
, so that
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{a}{{{}}a}:=a_{s}-a_{b}=a_{s}
. This function
a
is assumed to satisfy the following properties (cf., e.g.,
[
41
,
49
,
70
]
):

(2.2)

\displaystyle a\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}^{+}_{0}),

(2.3)

\displaystyle a>0\textup{ in accumulation areas within }\Omega_{t}^{+},

(2.4)

\displaystyle a\geq 0\textup{ in ablation areas within }\Omega_{t}^{+},

(2.5)

\displaystyle a<0\textup{ in }\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Omega-}{{{}}\Omega_{t}^{-}}:=\Omega\setminus(\Omega_{t}^{+}\cup\Gamma_{f,t}).

Ice sheets are characterised by incompressible, non-Newtonian, gravity-driven flows
[
33
,
41
]
, which transport ice from accumulation areas to ablation areas within
\Omega_{t}^{+}
.
The
basal sliding velocity
is a given horizontal vector field
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Ub}{{{}}\bm{U}_{b}}:\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
. For a
frozen
base (i.e., with bedrock temperature below the pressure melting point), it is reasonable to assume that
\bm{U}_{b}\equiv\bm{0}
(cf., e.g., Section 5.4 in
[
41
]
). The
horizontal ice flow velocity
is denoted by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{U}{{{}}\bm{U}}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and the
vertically integrated ice volume flux
is denoted and defined by:

(2.6)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Q}{{{}}\bm{Q}}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{2},\quad\bm{Q}:=\int_{0}^{h}\bm{U}\,\mathrm{d}z.

Following
[
49
]
, we assume no ice flux into the ice-free region
\Omega_{t}^{-}

\bm{Q}\cdot\bm{\nu}_{\Gamma_{f,t}}=0,\quad\textup{ on }\Gamma_{f,t},

where
ν

Γ

f
,
t

denotes the
outer unit normal vector to the free boundary
\Gamma_{f,t}
pointing towards the region of
\Omega
that is not covered with ice
. Consistently, we are in position to extend the volume flux by zero outside of
\Omega_{t}^{+}
:

(2.7)

\bm{Q}=\bm{0}\quad\textup{ in }\Omega_{t}^{-}.

Furthermore, as in
[
49
]
, we impose the boundary conditions

h(t,\cdot)=0\textup{ on }\Gamma_{f,t}\qquad\textup{ and }\qquad h(t,\cdot)=0\textup{ on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,

noting that
h(t,\cdot)
can be extended by zero in
\Omega_{t}^{-}
.

The ice thickness evolution is governed by the
ice thickness equation
(cf. equation (5.55) in
[
41
]
):

(2.8)

\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}=-\nabla\cdot\bm{Q}+a,\quad\textup{ in }\Omega_{t}^{+}\textup{ for a.a. }t\in(0,T).

A proper description of the free boundary
\Gamma_{f,t}
and the region
\Omega_{t}^{+}
requires a weak formulation of the problem. To formally derive the associated boundary value problem, we first multiply (
2.8
) by
(v-h)
for a sufficiently smooth test function
v
satisfying
v\geq 0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, and then integrate over
\Omega
. Using the decomposition
\Omega=\Omega_{t}^{+}\sqcup\Omega_{t}^{-}\sqcup\Gamma_{f,t}
, applying the Green formula (cf., e.g., Theorem 8.2-2 in
[
21
]
), incorporating conditions (
2.7
), resorting to the property that
h=0
in
\Omega_{t}^{-}
and – thus – that
\bm{Q}(t,\cdot)=\bm{0}
in
\Omega_{t}^{-}
, resorting to the property that
\frac{\partial h}{\partial t}(t,\cdot)\geq 0
in
\Omega_{t}^{-}
, and resorting to the properties of
a
(viz. (
2.2
)–(
2.5
)) leads to

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega}\bm{Q}\cdot\nabla(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\bm{Q}\cdot\nabla(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega_{t}^{-}}\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\Omega_{t}^{-}}\bm{Q}\cdot\nabla(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\Omega}a(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x,

for a.a.
t\in(0,T)
.
We thus obtained the following weak variational formulation associated with (
2.8
):
Find
h\geq 0
satisfying the variational inequalities

(2.9)

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\bm{Q}\cdot\nabla(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}a(v-h)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,

for all test functions
v
with
v\geq 0
a.e. in
(0,T)\times\Omega
, satisfying the boundary conditions

h=0\quad\textup{ on }(0,T)\times\partial\Omega,

and satisfying the initial condition

h(0,x)=h_{0}(x)\quad\textup{ for all }x\in\overline{\Omega},

where the initial datum
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{h0}{{{}}h_{0}}=h(0)
is given, non-negative, and not identically zero (cf. (
2.1
)).

Examples of initial values
h_{0}
could be, for instance, the Bueler or Vialov profile
[
41
]
.

(a)

Bueler profile.

(b)

Vialov profile.

Figure 1
.
Instances of Bueler profile and Vialov profile
[
41
]
.

The precise definition of a solution, including the regularity requirements for
h
and the data, will be addressed in Sections
4
and
5
.

Let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Tm}{{{}}\Theta_{\textup{m}}}>0
be a constant denoting
a temperature sufficiently below the pressure melting point
1
1

1

From the physical point of view, the pressure melting point represents a critical threshold which, when reached, latent heat is produced and the model describing the evolution of the ice surface elevation (viz., e.g., formula (5.98) in
[
41
]
) changes. Since the thermal model we will consider in Section
3
(viz. (
3.1
)) is not designed to take into account upper bounds for the temperature, we must enforce this constraint via the variational inequality approach.
.
For each
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}
, we define the function

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Tice}{{{}}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}(t,x,\cdot):[0,h(t,x)]\to[0,\Theta_{\textup{m}}],

which represents the
shallow ice sheet internal temperature at time
t
.
We denote by
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{A}{{{}}A}=A(x,z,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z))
the
ice softness
2
2

2

The function
A
is also known in the literature as
rate factor

[
34
,
41
]
.
and we observe that a widely accepted expression for
A
is given via the Arrhenius law (cf., e.g., Section 4.3 in
[
41
]
, or page 266 in
[
34
]
)

(2.10)

A(x,z,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z))=\max\left\{A_{0}\exp\left(-\beta\left(\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z)-\Theta_{\textup{m}}\right)\right),A_{\textup{min}}\right\},

for all
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and all
0\leq z\leq h(t,x)
, where
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Amin}{{{}}A_{\textup{min}}}>0
is the
minimal ice softness
3
3

3

In terrestrial ice sheets, temperatures range from about
200\degree\textup{K}
(Antarctic cold regions) to
273\degree\textup{K}
. Even at the coldest natural conditions,
A
remains positive but very small (e.g.,
\sim 10^{-28}\,\mathrm{Pa}^{-3}\,\mathrm{s}^{-1}
for
T=200\degree\textup{K}
)
[
49
]
.
, and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{A0}{{{}}A_{0}}\geq A_{\textup{min}}
and
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{beta}{{{}}\beta}>0
are positive parameters (cf., e.g. formula (14) in
[
34
]
).

We will see in the forthcoming construction of the model that the function
A
will provide the coupling with the internal ice temperature in the equations governing the evolution of the shallow ice sheet thickness.

Ice exhibits viscous behaviour, with its viscosity characterised by the Glen power law
[
39
]
(see also equation (4.16) in
[
41
]
). This law incorporates the ice softness parameter
A(x,z,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z))
and the
Glen exponent

p
, which satisfies
2.8\leq\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{p}{{{}}p}\leq 5
. The range of values for
p
is supported by laboratory experiments
[
40
]
. Treating the ice softness as a function enables the coupling of the ice thickness equation with a thermodynamic model
[
41
,
43
]
. From the point of view of ice modelling, the celebrated constitutive relations proposed by Hibler
[
42
]
, itself inspired by the article of Coon
[
22
]
, are
less accurate
in describing the horizontal ice flow velocity
\bm{U}
than Glen’s model, on the one hand. On the other hand, Hibler’s approach achieves the goal of describing in the same instance the twofold nature of ice as a solid and as a fluid.

According to equation (5.84) in
[
41
]
, the horizontal ice flow velocity
\bm{U}=(U_{1},U_{2})
can be expressed in terms of the upper ice surface elevation
h
via Glen’s power law
[
39
]

(2.11)

\bm{U}(t,x,z)=-2(\rho g)^{p-1}\left(\int_{0}^{z}A(x,s,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,s))(h(t,x)-s)^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)|\nabla h(t,x)|^{p-2}\nabla h(t,x),

for all
(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,h(t,x)]
, where
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{rho}{{{}}\rho}>0
is a constant describing the
ice mass density
, and
g
denotes the
gravitational acceleration
.

We note that formula (
2.11
) not only employs Glen’s power law, but is also valid only within a specific shallow limit derived from the Stokes model (see, for example,
[
70
]
). The
vertical ice flow velocity
(cf., e.g., formula (5.72) in
[
41
]
) is given by:

(2.12)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{vz}{{{}}v_{z}}(t,x,z):=-\int_{0}^{z}\nabla\cdot\bm{U}(t,x,s)\,\mathrm{d}s,\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,h(t,x)].

Substituting expression (
2.11
) into (
2.6
) gives:

(2.13)

\displaystyle\bm{Q}

\displaystyle=-2(\rho g)^{p-1}\left(\int_{0}^{h}\int_{0}^{z}A(\cdot,s,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(\cdot,\cdot,s))(h-s)^{p-1}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}z\right)|\nabla h|^{p-2}\nabla h

\displaystyle=-2(\rho g)^{p-1}\left(\int_{0}^{h}A(\cdot,s,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(\cdot,\cdot,s))(h-s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\right)|\nabla h|^{p-2}\nabla h,

where the latter equality is obtained by an integration by parts with respect to the variable
z
.

The weak formulation (
2.9
) constitutes a
degenerate extension
of the
p
-Laplacian obstacle problem because the ice thickness
h
approaches zero at the free boundary
\Gamma_{f,t}
for each
t\in[0,T]
. Consequently, solutions to (
2.9
) exhibit infinite gradients at the ice margin
\Gamma_{f,t}
, as discussed in
[
12
,
41
,
49
]
. The boundary degeneracy was verified by means of laboratory experiments, the results of which are reported in
[
67
]
.
A reformulation yielding a non-degenerate
p
-Laplacian structure is presented below (see (
3.25
)). Although this alternative formulation resolves the non-degeneracy, it would introduce a “tilt” that disrupts the monotonicity of the variational form. This is the very reason for which we limit ourselves to considering flat bedrocks in the context of this paper.

In order to overcome the difficulty arising as a result of the gradient degeneracy in the vicinity of the free boundary, we introduce the following
change of variables
, originally suggested by P.-A. Raviart in
[
64
]
(see also
[
15
]
):

(2.14)

h:=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{u}{{{}}u}^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}.

Observe that
h>0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
if and only if
u>0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, and that
h(t,x)=0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
if and only if
u=0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
. As a result of the transformation (
2.14
), we
first
obtain, formally,

(2.15)

\dfrac{\partial h}{\partial t}=\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(|u|^{\frac{3p-1}{2p}-2}\,u\right),

and,
secondly
, we obtain

(2.16)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{h}A(x,s,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,s))(h-s)^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=u^{\frac{(p+1)(p-1)}{2p}}\int_{0}^{1}A(x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\,s^{\prime},\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\,s^{\prime}))(1-s^{\prime})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime},

and,
third
, we obtain:

(2.17)

|\nabla h|^{p-2}\nabla h=\left(\dfrac{p-1}{2p}\right)^{p-1}u^{\frac{(-p-1)(p-1)}{2p}}|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u.

Define the function
\tilde{a}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by:

(2.18)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{atilde}{{{}}\tilde{a}}(t,x,u):=a\left(t,x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\right).

Inserting (
2.16
)–(
2.18
) into (
2.13
) gives:

(2.19)

-\bm{Q}=2\left(\rho g\dfrac{p-1}{2p}\right)^{p-1}\left(\int_{0}^{1}A\left(x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}s^{\prime},\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\,s^{\prime})\right)(1-s^{\prime})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\right)|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u.

For the sake of brevity, we define the function
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{mu}{{{}}\mu}:\overline{\Omega}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
as a function of
u
by:

(2.20)

\displaystyle\mu(x,u,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u)):=2\left(\rho g\dfrac{p-1}{2p}\right)^{p-1}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{0}^{1}A\left(x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}s^{\prime},\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\,s^{\prime})\right)(1-s^{\prime})^{p}\,\mathrm{d}s^{\prime}\right).

Since the ice softness
A
is of the form (
2.10
), we obtain that

(2.21)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{mu1}{{{}}\mu_{1}}:=2\left(\rho g\dfrac{p-1}{2p}\right)^{p-1}\dfrac{A_{\textup{min}}}{p+1}\leq\mu(x,u,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u))\leq 2\left(\rho g\dfrac{p-1}{2p}\right)^{p-1}\dfrac{A_{0}}{p+1}=:\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{mu2}{{{}}\mu_{2}},

for all
t\in[0,T]
and all
x\in\overline{\Omega}
. Observe that plugging (
2.20
) into (
2.19
) gives:

(2.22)

-\bm{Q}=\mu(x,u,\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,u))|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u.

Plugging (
2.15
)–(
2.18
) and (
2.22
) into (
2.9
) leads to the following weak formulation:
Find

u\geq 0

satisfying the variational inequality

(2.23)

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}\left(|u|^{\frac{3p-1}{2p}-2}\,u\right)\right)(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mu(x,u,\Theta_{\textup{ice}})|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\tilde{a}(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,

for all test functions

v

such that
v\geq 0
in
[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}
, satisfying the following boundary conditions

u(t,\cdot)=0,\quad\textup{ on }\partial\Omega\textup{ for all }t\in[0,T],

and satisfying the initial condition:

u(0,\cdot)=\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{u0}{{{}}u_{0}}=h_{0}^{\frac{2p}{p-1}},\quad\textup{ in }\overline{\Omega}.

3.
Formal derivation of the ice internal temperature evolution model

Let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{c}{{{}}c}>0
be a constant denoting the
ice specific heat
, and let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{kappa}{{{}}\kappa}>0
be a constant denoting the
ice heat conductivity
. The internal ice temperature evolution is governed by the following advection–diffusion thermal equation (viz. equation (5.105) in
[
41
]
)

(3.1)

\displaystyle\rho c\left(\dfrac{\partial\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}{\partial z}\right)

\displaystyle=\kappa\dfrac{\partial^{2}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}}{\partial z^{2}}+2(\rho g)^{p}A(x,z,\Theta_{\textup{ice}})(h-z)^{p}|\nabla h|^{p},

in
\Omega_{t}^{+}\times(0,h(t,x))
for all
t\in(0,T)
. We observe that (
3.1
) is posed over a domain that depends on the ice thickness
h
, rendering the problem under consideration a
moving boundary problem
(viz., e.g.,
[
24
,
59
]
). The advection term is expressed in terms of the ice velocity, whose components are given in (
2.11
) and (
2.12
).

We also assume, in agreement with Section 4.3.2 in
[
41
]
, that the viscosity has a finite limit as the effective stress tends to zero. This remark justifies the approximation:

(3.2)

|\nabla h|\approx C_{0},\quad\textup{ in }(0,T)\times\Omega_{t}^{+}\times(0,h(t,x)),

for some
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{C0}{{{}}C_{0}}>0
. Conceptually, this constant plays a role analogous to the regularisation parameter
\sigma_{0}
in the regularised Glen’s flow law (cf. e.g., Section 4.3.2 in
[
41
]
), ensuring the stress term remains bounded. We note that while approximation (
3.2
) removes the spatial variability of the surface slope, it retains the essential vertical structure of the shear stress through the
(h-z)
factor.

Since we are interested in studying the case where the bedrock is frozen, admissible ice internal temperatures vary in the following range:

(3.3)

0\degree\textup{K}<\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,0)\leq\Theta_{\textup{m}},\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}.

Let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Ts}{{{}}\Theta_{\textup{surf}}}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}
denote the
surface ice temperature
, which is the temperature measured on the surface of the ice sheet, and whose unit measure is
\degree\textup{K}
. Measurements have shown that
\Theta_{\textup{surf}}
can be well-approximated by the mean-annual surface air temperature, as long as the latter is not above
0\celsius
(viz. page 67 in
[
41
]
). We thus require the following condition to hold:

(3.4)

\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,h(t,x))=\Theta_{\textup{surf}}(t,x),\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}.

Let us denote by
q

geo

⟂

a function in
L^{2}(\Omega)
describing the
geothermal heat flux
injected from the bedrock into the ice. This function is positive everywhere on the bedrock (viz., e.g., formula (5.39) in
[
41
]
).

We denote by
ν

b

the unit vector normal to the bedrock which points towards the portion of three-dimensional space that
does not contain
the bedrock.
The surface ice temperature allows us to introduce a
thermodynamical boundary condition
for
\Theta_{\textup{ice}}
on the ice surface in contact with the air. For what concerns the ice surface in contact with the bedrock, the thermodynamical boundary condition that is to be considered in the case where the bedrock is frozen is given by (cf., e.g.,
[
41
]
):

(3.5)

\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{b}}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,0)=\dfrac{q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}}{\kappa},\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}.

We observe that the partial differential equation governing the evolution of the internal ice temperature (
3.1
) is characterised by a coupling with the ice thickness that is
stronger
than the coupling occurring in (
2.8
) when Glen’s power law is in place (viz. (
2.13
)).

The initial condition the ice internal temperature has to satisfy takes the form

(3.6)

\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(0,x,z)=\Theta_{0}(x,z),\quad\textup{ for all }(x,z)\in\overline{\Omega_{0}^{+}}\times[0,h_{0}(x)],

where the
initial shallow ice sheet internal temperature

\Theta_{0}
is given, and complies with (
3.3
) and (
3.5
).

The main difficulty in analysing (
3.1
) is due to the presence of a
moving boundary
, represented by the ice surface
h
, and by the presence of a free boundary, represented by the set
\Omega_{t}^{+}
. In order to render this Partial Differential Equation more tractable from the analytical point of view, we make additional assumptions on the given data and we recast (
3.1
)–(
3.6
) in a way that the temperature of the
whole observed environment
is taken into account. The strategy we are going to employ is inspired by the
Enthalpy Method

[
23
]
.

Denote by
ν

ice

the outer unit normal vector applied along the surface
\{(x,h(t,x));x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\}
and pointing towards to portion of space
complementary to the one occupied by ice
at time
t\in[0,T]
. We require that there exists a positive constant
r

0

such that:

(3.7)

\textup{dist}(\partial\Omega_{t}^{+},\partial\Omega)>r_{0},\quad\textup{ for all }t\in[0,T].

Note that (
3.7
) introduces a
restriction on the horizontal expansion
of the shallow ice sheet under consideration. More precisely, the assumption (
3.7
) means that
\textup{supp }h(t,\cdot)
is compact in
\Omega
for each
t\in[0,T]
. We denote by
L
a positive constant, associated with the maximum ice surface elevation, that we will
rigorously
determine in Section
4
.

Let
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{xi}{{{}}\xi}=\xi(t,x,r)
is a
smooth cut-off function
varying between 0 and 1, which is equal to one for
0\leq r\leq\frac{r_{0}}{2}
and is equal to zero for
\frac{3}{4}r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{0}
.

For a given
t\in[0,T]
and
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, define an
external auxiliary temperature

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Text}{{{}}\Theta_{\textup{ext}}}(t,x,\cdot):\{z\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\};h(t,x)\leq z\leq L\}
, where we recall that
h(t,x)
is identically zero for all
x\in\Omega_{t}^{-}
. For each
t\in[0,T]
,
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, and
0\leq r\leq r_{0}
, define:

(3.8)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,(x,h(t,x))+r\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}}(t,x))

\displaystyle:=\xi(t,x,r)\left\{\Theta_{\textup{surf}}(t,x)+\left[\Theta_{\textup{surf}}(t,x)-\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,(x,h(t,x))-r\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}}(t,x))\right]\right\}.

Note that
\xi\equiv 1
in a tubular neighbourhood of the ice surface
\{(x,h(t,x));x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\}
of thickness
r_{0}
, and smoothly decreases to zero moving further away. Additionally, we observe that
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
decreases moving far from the ice surface
\{(x,h(t,x));x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\}
.

Thanks to (
3.4
), it results that
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}
satisfies the following conditions for all
(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}
:

(3.9)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,h(t,x))=\Theta_{\textup{surf}}(t,x),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,h(t,x))=\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,h(t,x))=-\partial_{-\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,h(t,x)).

Thanks to (
3.8
)–(
3.10
), we infer that:

•

For each
t\in[0,T]
, the functions
\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
and
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
are glued continuously along the surface
\{(x,h(t,x));x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\}
;

•

For each
t\in[0,T]
, the normal derivatives of the functions
\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
and
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
do not have jumps along the surface
\{(x,h(t,x));x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\}
;

•

For each
t\in[0,T]
, the function
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,\cdot,\cdot)
has a controlled growth and decreases down to zero moving far from the ice surface. Note that the cut-off
\xi
has indeed been introduced for this purpose.

Starting from the definition and properties of
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}
listed beforehand (cf., (
3.8
)–(
3.10
)), we construct an extension of
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}
to the region containing ice, and we denote one such extension by
Θ

int

.
The function
\Theta_{\textup{int}}
is defined in a way that, in the region with ice, it is less or equal than
\Theta_{\textup{ice}}
at each time. For each
t\in[0,T]
, for each
x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}
, and for each
0\leq r\leq r_{0}
we define
\Theta_{\textup{int}}
close to the ice profile as follows

(3.11)

\Theta_{\textup{int}}(t,(x,h(t,x))-r\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)})=\xi(t,x,r)\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,(x,h(t,x))-r\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}),

where
\xi=\xi(t,x,z)
is the smooth cut-off function introduced beforehand, that varies between 0 and 1, and that is equal to one for
0\leq r\leq\frac{r_{0}}{2}
and is equal to zero for
\frac{3}{4}r_{0}\leq r\leq r_{0}
.

Remark 3.1
.

We notice that this construction holds for ice sheets exhibiting vertical slopes at the ice margins (i.e., close to
\Gamma_{f,t}
) like, for instance, the Bueler and Vialov profiles. However, the construction can be adapted to more general ice surfaces by taking
\Theta_{\textup{int}}\equiv\Theta_{\textup{surf}}
near the ice margins and applying another cut-off.

\blacksquare

Thanks to (
3.11
), we are able to infer that
\Theta_{\textup{int}}\equiv 0
in the interior of the ice region
sufficiently far away
from the ice surface, so that it results:

(3.12)

\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z)\geq\Theta_{\textup{int}}(t,x,z),\quad\textup{ for all }t\in[0,T]\textup{ for all }x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\textup{ and for all }0\leq z\leq h(t,x).

For each
t\in[0,T]
, we define the following
auxiliary temperature

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Taux}{{{}}\Theta_{\textup{aux}}}(t,x,z):=\begin{cases}\Theta_{\textup{int}}(t,x,z)&,\quad\textup{ if }x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\textup{ and }0\leq z\leq h(t,x),\\
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,z)&,\quad\textup{ if }x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{-}}\textup{ or }z>h(t,x).\end{cases}

Thanks to (
3.10
), it is then straightforward to observe that for each
t\in[0,T]
:

(3.13)

\displaystyle\Theta_{\textup{aux}}(t,x,h(t,x))=\Theta_{\textup{surf}}(t,x),\textup{ for all }x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{aux}}(t,x,h(t,x))=\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,h(t,x))\\
&=\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,h(t,x)),\textup{ for all }x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}.\end{aligned}

The latter conditions mean that, at each
t\in[0,T]
, the function
\Theta_{\textup{aux}}
is glued continuously along the curve
(x,h(t,x))
,
x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}
, that the normal derivative of
\Theta_{\textup{aux}}
has no jumps along the ice surface.

We now define the mapping
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Theta}{{{}}\Theta}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
as follows

(3.15)

\Theta(t,x,z):=\begin{cases}\Theta_{\textup{ice}}(t,x,z)&,\quad\textup{ if }t\in[0,T],x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}\textup{ and }0\leq z\leq h(t,x),\\
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}(t,x,z)&,\quad\textup{ if }t\in[0,T],x\in\overline{\Omega_{t}^{-}}\textup{ or }z>h(t,x),\end{cases}

and we define the mapping
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Thetatilde}{{{}}\tilde{\Theta}}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
as follows:

(3.16)

\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,z):=\Theta(t,x,z)-\Theta_{\textup{aux}}(t,x,z),\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L].

We then define the initial condition for
\tilde{\Theta}
as follows:

(3.17)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{T0tilde}{{{}}\tilde{\Theta}_{0}}(x,z):=\tilde{\Theta}(0,x,z)=\begin{cases}\Theta(0,x,z)-\Theta_{\textup{aux}}(0,x,z)&,\textup{ if }0<z<h_{0}(x),\\
0&,\textup{ otherwise}.\end{cases}

Remark 3.2
.

Observe that one could forge an
ad hoc

\tilde{\Theta}_{0}
that vanishes near the ice surface if
h_{0}
describes, for instance, the shape of a Bueler profile or a Vialov profile.

\blacksquare

Thanks to the properties of
\Theta_{\textup{ext}}
(see (
3.8
)–(
3.10
)), the properties of
\Theta_{\textup{int}}
(see (
3.11
)–(
3.12
)), and the properties of
\Theta_{\textup{aux}}
(see (
3.13
)–(
3.16
)), it results:

(3.18)

\displaystyle 0\leq\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,z)\leq\Theta_{\textup{m}},\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L],

(3.19)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,h(t,x))=0,\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}},

(3.20)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,z)=0,\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\partial\Omega\times[0,L],

(3.21)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,L)=0,\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega},

(3.22)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{b}}\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,0)=\dfrac{q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}}{\kappa},\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega},

(3.23)

\displaystyle\partial_{\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}(t,x)}}\tilde{\Theta}(t,x,h(t,x))=0,\quad\textup{ for all }(t,x)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega_{t}^{+}}.

Note that the Neumann boundary condition (
3.22
) accounts for an error deriving from the tubular neighbourhood of width
r_{0}
associated with
\Theta_{\textup{aux}}
. Since
r_{0}
is small, it is licit to perform one such approximation.

In light of (
3.16
), we recast the left-hand side of the thermal equation (
3.1
) in terms of
\tilde{\Theta}

(3.24)

\displaystyle\rho c\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)-\kappa\Delta\tilde{\Theta}

\displaystyle=2(\rho gC_{0})^{p}A(x,z,\tilde{\Theta})\left(\{h-z\}^{+}\right)^{p},

for all
(t,x,z)\in[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]
.

Remark 3.3

(Removal of the corrector near the ice surface)
.

Rather than extending (
3.1
) rigorously to the fixed domain, we are going to define a new approximate model on the fixed cylinder
\Omega\times(0,L)
. The temperature field
\tilde{\Theta}
satisfies (
3.24
) on the whole domain, with the source term supported only where ice is present. The moving boundary conditions are encoded in the auxiliary field
\Theta_{\textup{aux}}
, whose mismatch with the true solution is confined to a boundary layer of thickness
r_{0}
. For
r_{0}
sufficiently small compared to the ice thickness, the bulk temperature is well-approximated. The dependence of the approximate model on the parameter
r_{0}
still remains, as the initial condition is chosen in a way that it complies with the construction performed beforehand. The sharp-interface model is recovered, in the sense of formal asymptotics, in the limit
r_{0}\to 0
, though a rigorous justification of this limit is beyond the scope of the present work.

\blacksquare

We now recover a suitable variational formulation associated with (
3.1
), (
3.18
)–(
3.23
), and the initial condition (
3.17
). Before that, it is important to point out that the problem under consideration does not fall into the categories considered in the literature of moving interface problems.

Remark 3.4

(Classification of the variational problem)
.

The problem under consideration is
not
a Stefan problem
[
23
]
.
In the prototypical Stefan problem, the moving boundary velocity appears
directly
in the thermal boundary condition known as
Stefan condition
.

In the problem we are considering here, the surface motion comes from solving the shallow ice equation (
2.8
), which is a mechanical model coupled with a thermodynamical model, and
the Stefan condition is absent
. The surface boundary condition for temperature remains a Dirichlet or Neumann condition, but the location of that boundary evolves primarily as a result of snow accumulation or ablation.

The problem here considered is also of a different category from the problems discussed in the literature connected with the Cahn–Hilliard equation (see, for instance,
[
36
,
37
,
38
]
). Indeed, in the problem we are considering here,only one phase is taken into account, in the sense that
no model is prescribed in the portion of space complementary to the region occupied by ice
. In the problem considered here, the ice surface moves because mass is added or removed, or because ice flows at a rate depending on the ice temperature. In models of Cahn–Hilliard type, the interface is typically regarded as a known datum of the problem (cf., e.g.,
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
13
]
). Besides, the choice of considering an elliptic equation over moving domains is crucial to derive the energy estimates leading to the sharp interface model as a result of a formal asymptotic analysis
[
1
]
. In the model considered here, instead, the evolution of the ice surface (which plays a similar role to the one of the moving interfaces considered in
[
1
,
2
,
3
,
13
]
) is an unknown of the problem that we find by solving the evolution equation (
2.23
).

\blacksquare

For each
t\in[0,T]
, define the open set
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{Dice}{{{}}D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\subset\Omega\times(0,L)
as the
portion of three-dimensional space occupied by ice at time
t
, namely:

D_{\textup{ice}}(t):=\{(x,z)\in\Omega\times(0,L);0<z<h(t,x)\}.

Let us denote by
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
the complementary of the set
D_{\textup{ice}}(t)
in
\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]
. Observe that, since
\tilde{\Theta}\equiv 0
in
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
, we can extend
U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)
,
U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)
and
v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)
continuously in
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
.

Consider a test function
\Xi:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to[0,\Theta_{\textup{m}}]
satisfying (
3.18
), (
3.20
), (
3.21
), and test the left-hand side of (
3.24
) at
(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})
. On the one hand, the fact that
\tilde{\Theta}(t,\cdot,\cdot)\equiv 0
in
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
, the fact that
\frac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}(t,\cdot)\geq 0
in
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
, and the fact that
\Xi(t,\cdot,\cdot)\geq 0
in
(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}
modify (
3.24
) into:

\displaystyle\rho c\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\geq\rho c\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=2(\rho gC_{0})^{p}\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}A(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{\Theta})(h-z)^{p}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=2(\rho gC_{0})^{p}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}A(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{\Theta})(\{h-z\}^{+})^{p}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z.

On the one hand, an application of the positiveness of
q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}
gives that for all
t\in(0,T)
:

\displaystyle\rho c\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\rho c\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\rho c\iint_{(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z-\kappa\iint_{(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}}\Delta\tilde{\Theta}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle=\rho c\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\rho c\iint_{(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa\iint_{D_{\textup{ice}}(t)}\nabla\tilde{\Theta}\cdot\nabla(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z+\kappa\iint_{(D_{\textup{ice}}(t))^{\textup{c}}}\underbrace{\nabla\tilde{\Theta}}_{=\bm{0}\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-2}}\cdot\nabla(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\int_{\Omega^{+}_{t}}q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,0)-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,0))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\int_{\Omega^{+}_{t}}\underbrace{\nabla\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))\cdot\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}}}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-7}}\left(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\partial\Omega}\nabla\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,z)\cdot\bm{\nu}_{\partial\Omega}(\underbrace{\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,z)-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,z)}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-5}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{H}^{1}\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad-\kappa\int_{\Omega}\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}(\cdot,\cdot,L)(\underbrace{\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,L)}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-6}}-\underbrace{\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,L)}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-6}})\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa\int_{\Omega^{-}_{t}\times\{0\}}\underbrace{\nabla\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,0)\cdot\bm{\nu}_{b}}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-2}}\left(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,0)-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,0)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa\int_{\Omega^{+}_{t}}\underbrace{\nabla\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))\cdot\bm{\nu}_{\textup{ice}}}_{=0\textup{ by }\eqref{Theta-final-7}}\left(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,h(\cdot,\cdot))\right)\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\leq\rho c\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle\quad+\kappa\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\tilde{\Theta}\cdot\nabla(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z+\int_{\Omega}q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,0)-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,0))\,\mathrm{d}x.

In conclusion, the evolution of the ice temperature is a solution of the following variational problem:
Find
\tilde{\Theta}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to[0,\Theta_{\textup{m}}]
satisfying the variational inequalities

\displaystyle\rho c\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z

\displaystyle+\kappa\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\tilde{\Theta}\cdot\nabla(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z+\int_{\Omega}q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}(\Xi(\cdot,\cdot,0)-\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,0))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle\geq 2(\rho gC_{0})^{p}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}A(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{\Theta})\left(\left\{u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}-z\right\}^{+}\right)^{p}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z,

for all
\Xi:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to\mathbb{R}
such that
0\leq\Xi\leq\Theta_{\textup{m}}
, the boundary conditions

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}=0

\displaystyle,\quad\textup{ on }[0,T]\times\partial\Omega\times[0,L],

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,L)=0

\displaystyle,\quad\textup{ on }[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega},

and the initial condition:

\tilde{\Theta}(0,\cdot,\cdot)=\tilde{\Theta}_{0}(\cdot,\cdot),\quad\textup{ in }\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L].

Finally, we are able to write down the formal variational formulation of the thermo-mechanical problem under consideration:
Find
u:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
and
\tilde{\Theta}:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to[0,\Theta_{\textup{m}}]
satisfying the following variational inequalities

(3.25)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial}{\partial t}(|u|^{\frac{3p-1}{2p}-2}\,u)\right)(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\mu(x,u,\Theta_{\textup{ice}})|\nabla u|^{p-2}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\geq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\tilde{a}(v-u)\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

(3.26)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\rho c\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\left(\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial t}+U_{1}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{1}}+U_{2}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial x_{2}}+v_{z}(0,\cdot,\cdot)\dfrac{\partial\tilde{\Theta}}{\partial z}\right)(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\quad+\kappa\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\tilde{\Theta}\cdot\nabla(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}q_{\textup{geo}}^{\perp}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\\
&\geq 2(\rho gC_{0})^{p}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{0}^{L}\int_{\Omega}A(\cdot,\cdot,\tilde{\Theta})\left(\left\{u^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}-z\right\}^{+}\right)^{p}(\Xi-\tilde{\Theta})\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}z\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{aligned}

for all
v:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
and for all
\Xi:[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L]\to[0,\Theta_{\textup{m}}]
, the boundary conditions

(3.27)

\displaystyle u=0,\quad\textup{ on }[0,T]\times\partial\Omega,

(3.28)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}=0,\quad\textup{ on }[0,T]\times\partial\Omega\times[0,L],

(3.29)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(\cdot,\cdot,L)=0,\quad\textup{ on }[0,T]\times\overline{\Omega},

as well as the initial conditions

(3.30)

\displaystyle u(0,\cdot)=u_{0}(\cdot)=[h_{0}(\cdot)]^{\frac{2p}{p-1}},\quad\textup{ in }\overline{\Omega},

(3.31)

\displaystyle\tilde{\Theta}(0,\cdot,\cdot)=\tilde{\Theta}_{0}(\cdot,\cdot),\quad\textup{ in }\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L],

where
h_{0}>0
and
0\leq\tilde{\Theta}_{0}\leq\Theta_{\textup{m}}
are given (viz. Remark
3.2
).

Once again, note that, at least for the time being, a rigorous concept of solution has not been defined yet. This task will be carried out in Section
4
, in which the function spaces where the solutions are going to be sought will be defined as well as the requirements a function has to meet in order to be regarded as a solution.

4.
Weak formulation of the unilateral boundary value problem

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a Lipschitz domain. Let the parameter
p
satisfy
2.8\leq p\leq 5
, as suggested by experimental results (cf., e.g.,
[
39
,
41
,
49
,
63
]
).
For simplicity, we adopt the following assumptions (viz. Sections
2
and
3
):

(H1)

The bedrock
b
is flat in
\overline{\Omega}
;

(H2)

The function
\tilde{a}
is independent of the ice sheet height and belongs to
W^{1,p,\infty}(0,T;\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}))
;
4
4

4

Note that
(H\ref{H3})
is a particular case of (
2.2
).

(H3)

The initial ice velocity
(\bm{U}(0),v_{z}(0))
is of class
\bm{\mathcal{C}}^{0}(\overline{\Omega}\times[0,L])
and is non-identically zero.

Note that assumption
(H\ref{H1})
implies that
\bm{U}_{b}=\bm{0}
.
For brevity, define the number

(4.1)

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{physsymbols}{alpha}{{{}}\alpha}:=\dfrac{3p-1}{2p},

and note that
1<\alpha<2<p+1
. By the Rellich–Kondrašov theorem, the following chain of embeddings holds:

W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)\hookrightarrow\hookrightarrow\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{\Omega})\hookrightarrow L^{\alpha}(\Omega).

Define the number
L
as the
theoretical maximum height a shallow ice sheet can reach
. Several physical mechanisms impose certain bounds for the number
L
. Atmospherically, the saturation vapour pressure of water decreases exponentially with temperature via the Clausius–Clapeyron relation
[
72
]
.
The Antarctic Ice Sheet does not exceed
5\,\text{km}
in thickness
[
35
]
. Extrapolating these trends, an ice sheet growing substantially beyond
5\,\text{km}
would encounter an atmosphere nearly devoid of moisture, making further snowfall-driven growth physically impossible.

Define the set

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{mathsymbols}{Ksurf}{{{}}K_{\textup{surf}}}:=\{v\in W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega);v\geq 0\textup{ in }\overline{\Omega}\},

and observe that no upper bound has been imposed, despite the number
L
having been introduced precisely as a theoretical maximum thickness. The reason for this is twofold.

First, the ice surface elevation
h
(and, consequently, the transformed variable
u
) is independent of the vertical coordinate
z
. Indeed, since
L
denotes the height of the cylinder
\Omega\times(0,L)
where the ice internal temperature is defined, it results that
L
does not constrain the admissible values of
u
mathematically.

Second, the
physical
impossibility of an ice sheet exceeding the height
L
means that the temperature model is not expected to produce meaningful values for
z>L
.

Imposing an upper bound on
K_{\textup{surf}}
is thus unnecessary both for physical reasons and for mathematical consistency of the model.

Define the function space

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{mathsymbols}{V}{{{}}V}:=\{\Xi\in H^{1}(\Omega\times(0,L));\Xi=0\textup{ on }\partial\Omega\times(0,L)\cup\Omega\times\{L\}\},

and define the set

\lx@glossaries@gls@link{mathsymbols}{Ktemp}{{{}}K_{\textup{temp}}}:=\{\Xi\in V;0\leq\Xi\leq\Theta_{\textup{m}}\textup{ a.e. in }\Omega\times(0,L)\}.

The weak formulation of the unilateral boundary value problem and the definition of a solution are derived via a constructive proof based on the penalty method. The use of the penalty method for proving the existence of solutions to time-dependent contact problems was introduced by J.-L. Lions
[
53
]
. We now generalise a preparatory result, originally established in
[
63
]
for the space
L^{2}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
I
be a bounded open interval in
\mathbb{R}
, and let
\mathscr{O}\subset\mathbb{R}^{m}
, with
m\geq 1
an integer, be a bounded open set. Let
2\leq q\leq\infty
.
Then, the following results hold:

(a)

The operator
\lx@glossaries@gls@link{mathsymbols}{neg-part}{{{}}-\{\cdot\}^{-}}:L^{q}(\mathscr{O})\to L^{q}(\mathscr{O})
defined by

f\in L^{q}(\mathscr{O})\mapsto-\{f\}^{-}:=\min\{f,0\}\in L^{q}(\mathscr{O}),

is monotone, bounded and Lipschitz continuous with Lipschitz constant equal to one;

(b)

The Nemitskii operator associated with
-\{\cdot\}^{-}
, which is defined by

-\{f\}^{-}(t):=-\{f(t)\}^{-},\quad\textup{ for each }f\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O})),

is such that

-\{\cdot\}^{-}:\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))\to\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))

is continuous.

Proof.

Observe that, for all measurable functions
f:\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}
and all
g:\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}
, the following estimate holds

(4.2)

\left|\left(-\{f\}^{-}(x)\right)-\left(-\{g\}^{-}(x)\right)\right|=\left|\left(-\{f(x)\}^{-}\right)-\left(-\{g(x)\}^{-}\right)\right|\leq|f(x)-g(x)|,

for a.a.
x\in\mathscr{O}
. Additionally, it results that

(4.3)

\left(\left(-\{f(x)\}^{-}\right)-\left(-\{g(x)\}^{-}\right)\right)(f(x)-g(x))\geq\left(\{f(x)\}^{-}-\{g(x)\}^{-}\right)^{2},

for a.a.
x\in\mathscr{O}
.
The rest of the proof is divided into two parts, numbered
(i)
and
(ii)
.

(i)

Proof of
(a)
. To establish the boundedness of
-\{\cdot\}^{-}
, let
\mathscr{F}\subset L^{q}(\mathscr{O})
be a bounded set. If
2\leq q<\infty
, we observe that (
4.2
) gives:

\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|-\{f\}^{-}|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\leq\|f\|_{L^{q}(\mathscr{O})},\quad\textup{ for all }f\in\mathscr{F}.

If
q=\infty
, we obtain that:

\sup_{x\in\mathscr{O}}|-\{f(x)\}^{-}|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathscr{O})},\quad\textup{ for all }f\in\mathscr{F}.

The Lipschitz continuity of
(-\{\cdot\}^{-})
follows by (
4.2
); indeed:

\left\|(-\{f\}^{-})-(-\{g\}^{-})\right\|_{L^{q}(\mathscr{O})}\leq\|f-g\|_{L^{q}(\mathscr{O})},\quad\textup{ for all }f,g\in L^{q}(\mathscr{O}).

To establish the monotonicity of
-\{\cdot\}^{-}
, let us first observe that if
2\leq q\leq\infty
then the assumed boundedness of
\mathscr{O}
allows us to infer that

-\{f\}^{-}g\in L^{1}(\mathscr{O}),\quad\textup{ for all }f,g\in L^{q}(\mathscr{O}).

Combining the latter with (
4.3
) gives

\int_{\mathscr{O}}\left(\left(-\{f\}^{-}\right)-\left(-\{g\}^{-}\right)\right)(f-g)\,\mathrm{d}x\geq\left\|\left(-\{f\}^{-}\right)-\left(-\{g\}^{-}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}(\mathscr{O})}^{2}\geq 0,

for all
f,g\in L^{q}(\mathscr{O})
, for all
2\leq q\leq\infty
.

(ii)

Proof of
(b)
. To begin with, we check that, given
2\leq q<\infty
, if
f\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))
then
-\{f\}^{-}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))
. For each
s,t\in\overline{I}
, an application of (
4.2
) gives:

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|(-\{f(t)\}^{-})-(-\{f(s)\}^{-})|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)\leq\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f(t)-f(s)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)\to 0,\quad\textup{ as }s\to t.

To check the continuity of the operator
-\{\cdot\}^{-}
from
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))
to itself, let
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence that converges to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{q}(\mathscr{O}))
and observe that an application of (
4.2
) gives:

\max_{t\in\overline{I}}\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|(-\{f_{n}(t)\}^{-})-(-\{f(t)\}^{-})|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\leq\max_{t\in\overline{I}}\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}(t)-f(t)|^{q}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{1/q}\to 0,\quad\textup{ as }n\to\infty.

The case
q=\infty
follows by resorting to a similar strategy. To show that
f\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{\infty}(\mathscr{O}))
implies
-\{f\}^{-}\in\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{\infty}(\mathscr{O}))
, it suffices to observe that for each
s,t\in\overline{I}
, an application of (
4.2
) gives:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathscr{O}}|(-\{f(t)(x)\}^{-})-(-\{f(s)(x)\}^{-})|\leq\sup_{x\in\mathscr{O}}|f(t)(x)-f(s)(x)|\to 0,\quad\textup{ as }s\to t.

To check the continuity of the operator
-\{\cdot\}^{-}
from
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{\infty}(\mathscr{O}))
to itself, let
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence that converges to
f
in
\mathcal{C}^{0}(\overline{I};L^{\infty}(\mathscr{O}))
and observe that an application of (
4.2
) gives

\max_{t\in\overline{I}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathscr{O}}|(-\{f_{n}(t)(x)\}^{-})-(-\{f(t)(x)\}^{-})|\right)\leq\max_{t\in\overline{I}}\left(\sup_{x\in\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}(t)-f(t)|\right)\to 0,\quad\textup{ as }n\to\infty,

and the proof is complete.
∎

Remark 4.2
.

The properties established in Lemma
4.1
can be established verbatim for
{⋅}

+

.

\blacksquare

The next lemma shows that the mapping
t\in\mathbb{R}\mapsto t^{\beta}
, with
0<\beta<1
, is sub-additive.

Lemma 4.3

(Lemma 3.2 in
[
63
]
)
.

Let
0<\beta\leq 1
. Then

\left||x|^{\beta}-|y|^{\beta}\right|\leq|x-y|^{\beta},\quad\textup{ for all }x,y\in\mathbb{R}.

∎

The next lemma is a complement of Lemma
4.3
.

Lemma 4.4

(Lemma 3.3 in
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\sigma\geq 1
. Then:

|x-y|^{\sigma}\leq\left|x^{\sigma}-y^{\sigma}\right|,\quad\textup{ for all }x,y\geq 0.

∎

Thanks to Lemma
4.4
, it is possible to establish the following result, which will allow us to recover the validity of the initial conditions for (
3.25
).

Lemma 4.5

(Lemma 3.4 in
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
0<T<\infty
be given, let
1<\sigma<2
, and let
u\in L^{\infty}(0,T;L^{\sigma}(\Omega))
be such that:

\dfrac{\,\mathrm{d}}{\,\mathrm{d}t}\left(|u|^{\frac{\sigma-2}{2}}u\right)\in L^{2}(0,T;L^{2}(\Omega)).

Then, we have that
\left(|u|^{\frac{\sigma-2}{2}}u\right)\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega))
,
|u|\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];L^{\sigma}(\Omega))
, and
u\in\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];L^{\sigma}(\Omega))
.
∎

The following lemma leverages Lemma
4.5
for establishing finer convergence properties.

Lemma 4.6

(Lemma 3.6 in
[
63
]
)
.

Let
\Omega
be a Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
0<T<\infty
be given, let
1<\sigma<2
.
Let
\{u_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
be such that:

\left\{|u_{k}|^{\frac{\sigma-2}{2}}u_{k}\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\textup{ strongly converges in }\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];L^{2}(\Omega)).

Then
\{|u_{k}|\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
strongly converges in
\mathcal{C}^{0}([0,T];L^{\sigma}(\Omega))
.
∎

The proof establishing the existence of solutions relies on a compactness result proved by Dubinskii
[
29
]
(see also
[
5
]
for improvements and corrections), as well as on other results presented by P.-A. Raviart
[
65
]
, for which we give a different proof below.

Lemma 4.7
.

Let
1<r<\infty
and let
r^{\prime}
denote the Hölder conjugate exponent of
r
. The following inequalities hold

\displaystyle(|\xi|^{r-2}\xi-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta)\xi

\displaystyle\geq\dfrac{|\xi|^{r}-|\eta|^{r}}{r^{\prime}},

\displaystyle(|\xi|^{r-2}\xi-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta)(\xi-\eta)

\displaystyle\geq C\left(|\xi|^{(r-2)/2}\xi-|\eta|^{(r-2)/2}\eta\right)^{2},\textup{ for some }C=C(r)>0,

for all
\xi,\eta\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof.

We begin with the first inequality. Expanding the left-hand side gives

(|\xi|^{r-2}\xi-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta)\xi=|\xi|^{r}-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\xi.

Hence the desired inequality is equivalent to

|\xi|^{r}-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\xi\geq\frac{|\xi|^{r}-|\eta|^{r}}{r^{\prime}}\iff\frac{|\xi|^{r}}{r}+\frac{|\eta|^{r}}{r^{\prime}}\geq|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\xi,

where we have used the identity
1-\frac{1}{r^{\prime}}=\frac{1}{r}
. Now
|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\xi\leq|\eta|^{r-1}|\xi|
, and by Young’s inequality
[
73
]
with exponents
r
and
r^{\prime}
,

\frac{|\xi|^{r}}{r}+\frac{(|\eta|^{r-1})^{r^{\prime}}}{r^{\prime}}\geq|\xi||\eta|^{r-1}.

Since
(r-1)r^{\prime}=r
, we have
(|\eta|^{r-1})^{r^{\prime}}=|\eta|^{r}
, and the first inequality follows.

The second inequality is due to Tartar
5
5

5

It appears that this inequality was originally established by Luc Tartar. See
[
71
]
for another proof.
. We now give a different proof for the second inequality.
For
\xi\neq\eta
, define

F(\xi,\eta)=\dfrac{\left(|\xi|^{r-2}\xi-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\right)(\xi-\eta)}{\left(|\xi|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\xi-|\eta|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\eta\right)^{2}},

and observe that the denominator vanishes only when
\xi=\eta
since the map
t\mapsto|t|^{(r-2)/2}t
is strictly monotone. Hence
F
is well defined and continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(\xi,\eta):\xi=\eta\}
. The rest of the proof is divided into four steps, numbered
(i)
–
(iv)
.

(i)

The mapping
F
is homogeneous of degree zero
. For any
\lambda>0
, it results

F(\lambda\xi,\lambda\eta)=\dfrac{\left(|\lambda\xi|^{r-2}\lambda\xi-|\lambda\eta|^{r-2}\lambda\eta\right)(\lambda\xi-\lambda\eta)}{\left(|\lambda\xi|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\lambda\xi-|\lambda\eta|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\lambda\eta\right)^{2}}=\dfrac{\lambda^{r}\left(|\xi|^{r-2}\xi-|\eta|^{r-2}\eta\right)(\xi-\eta)}{\lambda^{r}\left(|\xi|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\xi-|\eta|^{\frac{r-2}{2}}\eta\right)^{2}}.

(ii)

Reduction of the problem to a compact set
.
Thanks to the homogeneity of
F
established in item
(i)
, the values of
F
on
\mathbb{R}^{2}\setminus\{(0,0)\}
are known by solely knowing the values of
F
on the compact set

S=\left\{(\xi,\eta):|\xi|^{r}+|\eta|^{r}=1\right\}.

Indeed, for
(\xi,\eta)\neq(0,0)
let
m=(|\xi|^{r}+|\eta|^{r})^{1/r}>0
. Then, it results
(\xi/m,\eta/m)\in S
and, thanks to item
(i)
,
F(\xi,\eta)=F(\xi/m,\eta/m)
.
The sought inequality certainly holds for
(\xi,\eta)=(0,0)
. Therefore, it suffices to establish the inequality in
S
and exploit the homogeneity of degree zero to conclude.

(iii)

The mapping
F
can be continuously extended to
S
. On
S
the denominator of
F
is zero exactly at the two points where
\xi=\eta
. On
S
the condition
\xi=\eta
implies
2|\xi|^{r}=1
, i.e.
\xi=\eta=\pm a
with
a=2^{-1/r}>0
.

We now show that
F
can be continuously extended to these two points, and that the extension attains positive values there.
Because of the evenness
F(-\xi,-\eta)=F(\xi,\eta)
, it is enough to study the limit as
(\xi,\eta)\to(a,a)
along
S
.

By homogeneity, for any
\eta>0
we have
F(\xi,\eta)=F(\xi/\eta,1)
. For points
(\xi,\eta)\in S
sufficiently close to
(a,a)
, both coordinates are positive, so we may reduce the limit to the behaviour of

H(\xi)=F(\xi,1)=\frac{(\xi^{r-1}-1)(\xi-1)}{(\xi^{r/2}-1)^{2}},\quad\textup{ for all }\xi>0\textup{ such that }\xi\neq 1.

As
(\xi,\eta)\to(a,a)
along
S
, the ratio
\xi/\eta\to 1
, and the limit of
F
on
S
coincides with
\lim_{\xi\to 1}H(\xi)
.
The Taylor expansion about
\xi_{0}=1
of the first factor in the numerator of
H
is given by:

\xi^{r-1}-1=(r-1)(\xi-1)+O((\xi-1)^{2}).

The Taylor expansion about
\xi_{0}=1
of the denominator of
H
is given by:

\xi^{r/2}-1=\dfrac{r}{2}(\xi-1)+O((\xi-1)^{2}).

Plugging these expressions into the expression for
H
gives:

H(\xi)=\dfrac{(r-1)(\xi-1)^{2}+O((\xi-1)^{3})}{\frac{r^{2}}{4}(\xi-1)^{2}+O((\xi-1)^{3})}\to\dfrac{4(r-1)}{r^{2}}>0\quad\textup{ as }\xi\to 1.

(iv)

Completion of the proof
. Since both maps
t\mapsto|t|^{r-2}t
and
t\mapsto|t|^{(r-2)/2}t
are strictly increasing for
r>1
, the numerator and denominator of
F
share the sign of the term
(\xi-\eta)
. Consequently
F>0
whenever
\xi\neq\eta
. The continuous extension of
F
to the diagonal takes the value
\frac{4(r-1)}{r^{2}}>0
. Thus
F
is everywhere strictly positive on the compact set
S
, and by continuity of the extension and compactness of
S
, the minimum of
F
on
S
exists and is strictly positive. We denote this minimum value by
C(r)
, and the proof is complete.
∎

We now establish two lemmas of general interest.

Lemma 4.8
.

The following estimate holds for all
1\leq r<\infty
:

|x^{r}-y^{r}|\leq r(x+y)^{r-1}|x-y|,\quad\textup{ for all }0\leq x,y<\infty.

Proof.

The case
x=y
is trivial, so we focus our attention on the case where
x\neq y
.
Since the mapping
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}\to\xi^{r}
is continuous and differentiable, an application of the Mean Value Theorem (cf., e.g., Theorem 9.3-1 in
[
21
]
) gives that there exists a number
\min\{x,y\}<\eta<\max\{x,y\}
such that:

x^{r}-y^{r}=r\eta^{r-1}(x-y).

Since
\eta<x+y
, the sought inequality immediately follows.
∎

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
m\geq 1
be an integer, let
\mathscr{O}
be an open subset of
\mathbb{R}^{m}
, let
1\leq r<\infty
, and let
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of non-negative functions
f_{n}:\mathscr{O}\to\mathbb{R}^{+}\cup\{0\}
. Then, then following results hold:

(a)

If the sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{r}(\mathscr{O})
as
n\to\infty
, then the sequence
\{f_{n}^{r}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{1}(\mathscr{O})
as
n\to\infty
;

(b)

If the sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(\mathscr{O})
as
n\to\infty
, with
1\leq p<\infty
, then the sequence
\{f_{n}^{r}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p/r}(\mathscr{O})
as
n\to\infty
provided that
1\leq r\leq p
.

Proof.

The proof is divided into two parts, numbered
(i)
and
(ii)
.

(i)

Proof of
(a)
. Let us denote the limit of the sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
L^{r}(\mathscr{O})
by
f
, and observe that
f\geq 0
a.e. in
\mathscr{O}
. If
r=1
the conclusion is trivial. If
r>1
, an application of Lemma
4.8
gives:

\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}^{r}-f^{r}|\leq r\int_{\mathscr{O}}(f_{n}+f)^{r-1}|f_{n}-f|\,\mathrm{d}x.

It is straightforward to observe that the sequence
\left\{(f_{n}+f)^{r-1}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is bounded in
L^{r^{\prime}}(\mathscr{O})
, where
r^{\prime}
is the Hölder conjugate exponent, independently of
n
. An application of Hölder’s inequality gives:

\int_{\mathscr{O}}(f_{n}+f)^{r-1}|f_{n}-f|\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}(f_{n}+f)^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{r-1}{r}}\left(\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}-f|^{r}\,\mathrm{d}x\right)^{\frac{1}{r}}.

Observing that the right-hand side of the latter is the product between a factor that is bounded independently of
n
and a factor that tends to zero as
n\to\infty
the conclusion follows.

(ii)

Proof of
(b)
. Let us denote the limit of the sequence
\{f_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
in
L^{p}(\mathscr{O})
by
f
, and observe that
f\geq 0
a.e. in
\mathscr{O}
. Since
p/r\geq 1
, an application of Lemma
4.4
and item
(a)
gives

\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}^{r}-f^{r}|^{p/r}\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\mathscr{O}}|f_{n}^{p}-f^{p}|\,\mathrm{d}x\to 0,\quad\textup{ as }n\to\infty,

and the proof is complete.
∎

We now generalise the conclusion in Lemma
4.6
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Let
0<T<\infty
, let
\Omega
be a Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
2.8\leq p\leq 5
and let
\alpha=\frac{3p-1}{2p}
. Let
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of functions
u_{n}:(0,T)\to W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be such that
\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{n}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{r}(0,T;L^{2p/\alpha}(\Omega))
,
for all
1\leq r<\infty
. Then, the sequence
\{|u_{n}|\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
.

Proof.

We show that the sequence
\{|u_{n}|\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is a Cauchy sequence in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
. To this aim, observe that an application of Lemma
4.4
gives:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left||u_{n}|-|u_{m}|\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left|\left||u_{n}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{n}\right|^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}-\left||u_{m}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{m}\right|^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}\right|^{p}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left|\left||u_{n}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{n}\right|^{\frac{2p}{\alpha}}-\left||u_{m}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{m}\right|^{\frac{2p}{\alpha}}\right|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{n}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
strongly converges in
L^{r}(0,T;L^{2p/\alpha}(\Omega))
for each
1\leq r<\infty
, an application of Lemma
4.9
(a)
with
r=2p/\alpha>4
gives that the sequence
\left\{\left||u_{n}|^{\frac{\alpha-2}{2}}u_{n}\right|^{\frac{2p}{\alpha}}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
strongly converges in
L^{1}((0,T)\times\Omega)
and so in
L^{1}(0,T;L^{1}(\Omega))
(Theorem 8.28 of
[
52
]
). Since, once again thanks to Theorem 8.28 of
[
52
]
, it is possible to identify
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
with
L^{p}((0,T)\times\Omega)
, the conclusion follows.
∎

Let us now study the convergence of the sequence
\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, for
2.8\leq p\leq 5
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Let
0<T<\infty
, let
\Omega
be a Lipschitz domain in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, let
2.8\leq p\leq 5
. Let
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of functions
u_{n}:(0,T)\to W_{0}^{1,p}(\Omega)
be such that
\{|u_{n}|\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
to a function
u
. Then, the sequence
\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly to
|u|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}
in
L^{2p^{2}/(p-1)}(0,T;L^{2p^{2}/(p-1)}(\Omega))
.

Proof.

We adopt the same strategy as Lemma
4.10
.
Since
2p^{2}/(p-1)>8
, an application of Lemma
4.4
gives:

\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left||u_{n}|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}-|u|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}\right|^{\frac{2p^{2}}{p-1}}\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left||u_{n}|^{p}-|u|^{p}\right|\,\mathrm{d}x\,\mathrm{d}t.

The conclusion follows as a result of an application of Lemma
4.9
(a)
and Theorem 8.28 of
[
52
]
.
∎

Thanks to the continuity of the positive part
\{\cdot\}^{+}
, an application of Lemma
4.1
(a)
(which clearly holds for
\{\cdot\}^{+}
as well in light of Remark
4.2
) gives that if
\{|u_{n}|\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
then:

\left\{\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}-z\right\}^{+}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\textup{ converges strongly in }L^{\frac{2p^{2}}{p-1}}((0,T)\times\Omega\times(0,L)).

Therefore, an application of Lemma
4.9
(b)
(observe that
\frac{5}{2}\leq\frac{2p}{p-1}\leq\frac{28}{9}
if
2.8\leq p\leq 5
) gives that if
\{|u_{n}|\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
converges strongly in
L^{p}(0,T;L^{p}(\Omega))
then:

(4.4)

\left\{\left(\left\{|u_{n}|^{\frac{p-1}{2p}}-z\right\}^{+}\right)^{p}\right\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\textup{ converges strongly in }L^{\frac{2p}{p-1}}((0,T)\times\Omega\times(0,L)).

For the purpose of studying the right-hand side in (
3.26
), we establish the following results of general interest.

Lemma 4.12
.

Let
\mathscr{O}
be a bounded open set in
\mathbb{R}^{m}
for some integer
m\geq 1
, let
2.8\leq p\leq 5
and let
\frac{10}{3}\leq q\leq\frac{28}{5}
. Let
\sigma\in\mathbb{R}
be defined by:

\dfrac{1}{\sigma}=\dfrac{1}{q}+\dfrac{p-1}{2p}.

Then, it results
q^{\prime}\leq\sigma\leq 2
and
2\leq\sigma^{\prime}\leq q
and the following chain of embeddings holds:
